 \text{there exists }(\beta,A,\mu)\text{ inducing a law in }\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}},\\
 A\neq0,\quad \operatorname{diag}(A)=0,\\
 \operatorname{spec}\!\left(\left.\Delta(A-\beta I_p)\right|_{\operatorname{im}(A)}\right)
 \subset\{z\in\mathbb C:|\operatorname{Im}z|<\pi\},\\
 \text{and }m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2\text{ are that law's population moments}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]
The restriction is well defined because \(\operatorname{im}(A)\) is invariant under \(A-\beta I_p\).  This is an image-defined domain; it does not require \(A\) to be invertible, acyclic, or free of reciprocal pairs, and it does not encode a recovered parameter as an observable premise.
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Mstable}; \texttt{m}; \texttt{Gamma}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:restricted-rank-two-lag-inverse]\label{def:restricted-rank-two-lag-inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Domain-\allowbreak{}restricted singular-\allowbreak{}feedback two-\allowbreak{}lag inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} For \(\eta=(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\in\mathfrak I_{\Delta,p}^{0,+,\mathrm{rs}}\), set
\[
 r=\operatorname{rank}(\Gamma_1),\quad S=\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1),
\]
choose any \(O\in\mathbb R^{p\times r}\) with \(O^{\mathsf T}O=I_r\) and \(\operatorname{im}(O)=S\), and define
\[
 R=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_1,\quad
 F=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_2R^{\mathsf T}(RR^{\mathsf T})^{-1},
\]
\[
 \lambda^\sharp=m/\Delta,\quad D^\sharp=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda^\sharp),
 \quad L^\sharp=\Delta^{-1}\operatorname{Log}(F),
\]
\[
 \beta^\sharp=-\operatorname{tr}(L^\sharp)/r,\quad
 A_S^\sharp=L^\sharp+\beta^\sharp I_r,\quad
 H^\sharp=L^\sharp(F-I_r)^{-1},
\]
\[
 K^\sharp=O(H^\sharp)^2R,\quad
 Z^\sharp=K^\sharp-(K^\sharp)^{\mathsf T},\quad
 P^\sharp=OO^{\mathsf T},\quad
 W^\sharp=O^{\mathsf T}(D^\sharp)^{-1}O,
\]
\[
 \mathsf C^\sharp=
 \{OA_S^\sharp-Z^\sharp(I_p-P^\sharp)(D^\sharp)^{-1}O\}
 (W^\sharp)^{-1}O^{\mathsf T}+Z^\sharp(I_p-P^\sharp),
\]
and
\[
 A^\sharp=\mathsf C^\sharp(D^\sharp)^{-1},\quad
 \mu^\sharp=(I_p-A^\sharp/\beta^\sharp)\lambda^\sharp.
\]
Define the domain-restricted map
\[
 \Phi_{\Delta,p}^{0,+,\mathrm{rs}}:\mathfrak I_{\Delta,p}^{0,+,\mathrm{rs}}
 \longrightarrow\mathbb R_+\times\mathbb R^{p\times p}\times\mathbb R^p,
 \quad
 \Phi_{\Delta,p}^{0,+,\mathrm{rs}}(\eta)
 =(\beta^\sharp,A^\sharp,\mu^\sharp).
\]
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Irestricted}; \texttt{m}; \texttt{Gamma}; \texttt{Delta}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:full-rank-stable-covariance-image]\label{def:full-rank-stable-covariance-image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Full-\allowbreak{}rank stable covariance-\allowbreak{}only population image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} Let \(\mathfrak J_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{fr}}\) be the population covariance image
\[
 \left\{(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2):\begin{array}{l}
 \text{there exists }(\beta,A,\mu)\text{ inducing a law in }\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}},\\
 \det(A)\neq0,\quad
 \operatorname{spec}\{\Delta(A-\beta I_p)\}
 \subset\{z\in\mathbb C:|\operatorname{Im}z|<\pi\},\\
 \text{and }\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2\text{ are that law's population covariances}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]
This is a population image under \((\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\), not a collection defined by observable gate conditions.
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Mstable}; \texttt{Gamma}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:full-rank-covariance-only-inverse]\label{def:full-rank-covariance-only-inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Domain-\allowbreak{}restricted full-\allowbreak{}rank covariance-\allowbreak{}only inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} For \(\gamma=(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\in\mathfrak J_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{fr}}\), set
\[
 T^0=\Gamma_2\Gamma_1^{-1},\qquad
 B^0=\Delta^{-1}\operatorname{Log}(T^0),\qquad
 J^0=(B^0)^{-1}(T^0-I_p),\qquad
 K^0=(J^0)^{-2}\Gamma_1,
\]
\[
 \widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{fr}}(t)=e^{B^0t}K^0,\qquad
 D^0=\Delta^{-1}\!\left[
 \Gamma_0-\int_0^\Delta(\Delta-t)
 \{\widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{fr}}(t)
   +\widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{fr}}(t)^{\mathsf T}\}\,dt\right],
\]
and
\[
 \lambda^0=\operatorname{diag}(D^0),\qquad m^0=\Delta\lambda^0.
\]
Define the domain-restricted covariance-only map by
\[
 \Psi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{fr},0}(\gamma)
 =\Phi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{fr}}(m^0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2).
\]
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{IfrCov}; \texttt{Gamma}; \texttt{Delta}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:restricted-rank-stable-covariance-image]\label{def:restricted-rank-stable-covariance-image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Restricted-\allowbreak{}rank stable covariance-\allowbreak{}only population image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} Let \(\mathfrak J_{\Delta,p}^{0,+,\mathrm{rs}}\) be the population covariance image
\[
 \left\{(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2):\begin{array}{l}
 \text{there exists }(\beta,A,\mu)\text{ inducing a law in }\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}},\\
 A\neq0,\quad \operatorname{diag}(A)=0,\\
 \operatorname{spec}\!\left(
 \left.\Delta(A-\beta I_p)\right|_{\operatorname{im}(A)}\right)
 \subset\{z\in\mathbb C:|\operatorname{Im}z|<\pi\},\\
 \text{and }\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2\text{ are that law's population covariances}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]
This is a population image under \((\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\), not a collection defined by observable gate conditions.  It permits singular excitation, directed cycles, reciprocal pairs, and isolated labels.
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Mstable}; \texttt{Gamma}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:restricted-rank-covariance-only-inverse]\label{def:restricted-rank-covariance-only-inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Domain-\allowbreak{}restricted singular-\allowbreak{}feedback covariance-\allowbreak{}only inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} For \(\gamma=(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\in
\mathfrak J_{\Delta,p}^{0,+,\mathrm{rs}}\), set
\[
 r=\operatorname{rank}(\Gamma_1),\qquad S=\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1),
\]
choose any \(O\in\mathbb R^{p\times r}\) with \(O^{\mathsf T}O=I_r\) and \(\operatorname{im}(O)=S\), and set
\[
 R=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_1,\qquad
 F=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_2R^{\mathsf T}(RR^{\mathsf T})^{-1},
\]
\[
 L^\sharp=\Delta^{-1}\operatorname{Log}(F),\qquad
 H^\sharp=L^\sharp(F-I_r)^{-1},\qquad
 \widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{rs}}(t)
 =Oe^{L^\sharp t}(H^\sharp)^2R,
\]
\[
 D^0=\Delta^{-1}\!\left[
 \Gamma_0-\int_0^\Delta(\Delta-t)
 \{\widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{rs}}(t)
  +\widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{rs}}(t)^{\mathsf T}\}\,dt\right],
 \qquad \lambda^0=\operatorname{diag}(D^0),
 \qquad m^0=\Delta\lambda^0.
\]
Define
\[
 \Psi_{\Delta,p}^{0,+,\mathrm{rs},0}(\gamma)
 =\Phi_{\Delta,p}^{0,+,\mathrm{rs}}(m^0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2).
\]
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{IrestrictedCov}; \texttt{Gamma}; \texttt{Delta}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:isolated-invertible-block-stable-moment-image]\label{def:isolated-invertible-block-stable-moment-image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Isolated invertible-\allowbreak{}block stable population moment image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} Let \(\mathfrak I_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib}}\) be the population moment image
\[
 \left\{(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2):\begin{array}{l}
 \text{there exist }(\beta,A,\mu)\text{ inducing a law in }\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}}\text{ and a set }I\subsetneq\{1,\ldots,p\},\\
 1\leq r=|I|\leq p-1,\quad E_I=(e_{i_1},\ldots,e_{i_r})\text{ for }i_1<\cdots<i_r\text{ the elements of }I,\\
 A=E_IA_IE_I^{\mathsf T},\quad A_I\in\mathbb R^{r\times r},\quad A_I\geq0,\quad \det(A_I)\neq0,\quad \rho(A_I)<\beta,\\
 \operatorname{spec}\{\Delta(A_I-\beta I_r)\}\subset\{z\in\mathbb C:|\operatorname{Im}z|<\pi\},\\
 \text{and }m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2\text{ are that law's population moments}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]
The inequalities \(A_I\geq0\) are entrywise.  Labels outside \(I\) retain their strictly positive baseline intensities but have no incoming or outgoing excitation.  This is an image-defined domain: no claim is made that membership in it can be certified from an arbitrary observed triple beyond the recovery statements proved on the image.
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Mstable}; \texttt{m}; \texttt{Gamma}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:isolated-invertible-block-two-lag-inverse]\label{def:isolated-invertible-block-two-lag-inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Isolated invertible-\allowbreak{}block two-\allowbreak{}lag inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} For \(\eta=(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\in\mathfrak I_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib}}\), set
\[
 S=\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1),\qquad
 I^\circ=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}:e_i\in S\},\qquad
 r^\circ=|I^\circ|,
\]
and let \(E^\circ=E_{I^\circ}\) be the ordered coordinate selector.  With \(\Gamma_1^\dagger\) denoting the Moore--Penrose inverse, define
\[
 F^\circ=(E^\circ)^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_2\Gamma_1^\dagger E^\circ,\qquad
 L^\circ=\Delta^{-1}\operatorname{Log}(F^\circ),\qquad
 J^\circ=\int_0^\Delta e^{L^\circ u}\,du,
\]
\[
 G_1^\circ=(E^\circ)^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_1E^\circ,\qquad
 K^\circ=(J^\circ)^{-2}G_1^\circ,\qquad
 \lambda^\circ=m/\Delta,\qquad
 D_I^\circ=(E^\circ)^{\mathsf T}\operatorname{diag}(\lambda^\circ)E^\circ,
\]
\[
 Q^\circ=\int_0^\infty e^{L^\circ u}D_I^\circ e^{(L^\circ)^{\mathsf T}u}\,du,\qquad
 s^\circ=(r^\circ)^{-1}\operatorname{tr}\{K^\circ(Q^\circ)^{-1}+(L^\circ)^2\},
\]
\[
 \beta^\circ=(s^\circ)^{1/2},\qquad
 A^\circ=E^\circ(L^\circ+\beta^\circ I_{r^\circ})(E^\circ)^{\mathsf T},\qquad
 \mu^\circ=(I_p-A^\circ/\beta^\circ)\lambda^\circ.
\]
Here \((J^\circ)^{-2}=(J^\circ)^{-1}(J^\circ)^{-1}\), as required by the positive-lag identity \(\Gamma_1=J^2K\).  Define
\[
 \Phi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib}}:\mathfrak I_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib}}
 \longrightarrow\mathbb R_+\times\mathbb R^{p\times p}\times\mathbb R^p,
 \qquad
 \Phi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib}}(\eta)=(\beta^\circ,A^\circ,\mu^\circ).
\]
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Iisolated}; \texttt{m}; \texttt{Gamma}; \texttt{Delta}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:isolated-invertible-block-stable-covariance-image]\label{def:isolated-invertible-block-stable-covariance-image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Isolated invertible-\allowbreak{}block stable covariance population image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} Let \(\mathfrak J_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib}}\) be the population covariance image
\[
 \left\{(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2):\begin{array}{l}
 \text{there exist }(\beta,A,\mu)\text{ inducing a law in }\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}}\text{ and a set }I\subsetneq\{1,\ldots,p\},\\
 1\leq r=|I|\leq p-1,\quad E_I=(e_{i_1},\ldots,e_{i_r})\text{ for }i_1<\cdots<i_r\text{ the elements of }I,\\
 A=E_IA_IE_I^{\mathsf T},\quad A_I\in\mathbb R^{r\times r},\quad A_I\geq0,\quad \det(A_I)\neq0,\quad \rho(A_I)<\beta,\\
 \operatorname{spec}\{\Delta(A_I-\beta I_r)\}\subset\{z\in\mathbb C:|\operatorname{Im}z|<\pi\},\\
 \text{and }\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2\text{ are that law's population covariances}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]
The inequalities \(A_I\geq0\) are entrywise.  This is a population image under \((\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\), not an observable gate imposed on arbitrary covariance triples.
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Mstable}; \texttt{Gamma}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:isolated-invertible-block-covariance-only-inverse]\label{def:isolated-invertible-block-covariance-only-inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Isolated invertible-\allowbreak{}block covariance-\allowbreak{}only inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} For \(\gamma=(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\in\mathfrak J_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib}}\), set
\[
 S=\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1),\qquad
 I^0=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}:e_i\in S\},\qquad
 r^0=|I^0|,
\]
and let \(E^0=E_{I^0}\) be the ordered coordinate selector.  Define
\[
 F^0=(E^0)^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_2\Gamma_1^\dagger E^0,\qquad
 L^0=\Delta^{-1}\operatorname{Log}(F^0),\qquad
 J^0=\int_0^\Delta e^{L^0u}\,du,
\]
\[
 G_1^0=(E^0)^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_1E^0,\qquad
 K^0=(J^0)^{-2}G_1^0,\qquad
 \widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{ib}}(t)=E^0e^{L^0t}K^0(E^0)^{\mathsf T},
\]
\[
 D^0=\Delta^{-1}\!\left[\Gamma_0-
 \int_0^\Delta(\Delta-t)
 \{\widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{ib}}(t)+\widetilde C_{+}^{\mathrm{ib}}(t)^{\mathsf T}\}\,dt\right],
 \qquad \lambda^0=\operatorname{diag}(D^0),
 \qquad m^0=\Delta\lambda^0.
\]
Define
\[
 \Psi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib},0}(\gamma)
 =\Phi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{ib}}(m^0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2).
\]
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{IisolatedCov}; \texttt{Gamma}; \texttt{Delta}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:complete-hawkes-response]\label{def:complete-hawkes-response}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Complete Hawkes conditional response in future-\allowbreak{}row orientation}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} For a law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}}\), define its complete nonsingular conditional-response matrix by
\[
 g(t)=
 \begin{cases}
 C_{+}(t)D^{-1},&t>0,\\
 C_{+}(-t)^{\mathsf T}D^{-1},&t<0,\\
 0,&t=0.
 \end{cases}
\]
The value at zero is immaterial for convolution and integration.  The normalization is on the right because \(C_{+}(t)_{ij}=\operatorname{Cov}(dN_i(t),dN_j(0))/dt^2=g^{ij}(t)\lambda_j\).
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Ccov}; \texttt{D}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:stable-nonzero-restricted-strip-moment-image]\label{def:stable-nonzero-restricted-strip-moment-image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Stable nonzero restricted-\allowbreak{}strip population moment image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} Let \(\mathfrak I_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH}}\) be the population moment image
\[
 \left\{(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2):\begin{array}{l}
 \text{there exists }(\beta,A,\mu)\text{ inducing a law in }\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}},\\
 A\neq0,\qquad
 \operatorname{spec}\!\left(\left.\Delta(A-\beta I_p)\right|_{\operatorname{im}(A)}\right)
 \subset\{z\in\mathbb C:|\operatorname{Im}z|<\pi\},\\
 \text{and }m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2\text{ are that law's population moments}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]
This image definition imposes no full-rank, zero-diagonal, coordinate-support, isolated-label, acyclicity, or invertible-active-block restriction.
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Mstable}; \texttt{m}; \texttt{Gamma}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:stable-nonzero-restricted-strip-covariance-image]\label{def:stable-nonzero-restricted-strip-covariance-image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Stable nonzero restricted-\allowbreak{}strip covariance population image}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} Let \(\mathfrak J_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH}}\) be the population covariance image
\[
 \left\{(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2):\begin{array}{l}
 \text{there exists }(\beta,A,\mu)\text{ inducing a law in }\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}},\\
 A\neq0,\qquad
 \operatorname{spec}\!\left(\left.\Delta(A-\beta I_p)\right|_{\operatorname{im}(A)}\right)
 \subset\{z\in\mathbb C:|\operatorname{Im}z|<\pi\},\\
 \text{and }\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2\text{ are that law's population covariances}
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]
This is the covariance-only image of exactly the same nonempty stable restricted-strip regime as \(\mathfrak I_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH}}\).
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Mstable}; \texttt{Gamma}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:wiener-hopf-two-lag-inverse]\label{def:wiener-hopf-two-lag-inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Broad stable two-\allowbreak{}lag Wiener-\allowbreak{}-\allowbreak{}Hopf inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} For \(\eta=(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\in\mathfrak I_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH}}\), set
\[
 r=\operatorname{rank}(\Gamma_1),\qquad S=\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1),
\]
choose any \(O\in\mathbb R^{p\times r}\) with \(O^{\mathsf T}O=I_r\) and \(\operatorname{im}(O)=S\), and define
\[
 R=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_1,\quad
 F=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_2R^{\mathsf T}(RR^{\mathsf T})^{-1},
\]
\[
 L^{\mathrm{WH}}=\Delta^{-1}\operatorname{Log}(F),\quad
 H^{\mathrm{WH}}=L^{\mathrm{WH}}(F-I_r)^{-1},\quad
 P^{\mathrm{WH}}=(H^{\mathrm{WH}})^2R,
\]
\[
 K^{\mathrm{WH}}=OP^{\mathrm{WH}},\qquad
 C_{+}^{\mathrm{WH}}(t)=Oe^{L^{\mathrm{WH}}t}P^{\mathrm{WH}},\qquad t>0.
\]
Set \(\lambda^{\mathrm{WH}}=m/\Delta\), \(D^{\mathrm{WH}}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda^{\mathrm{WH}})\), and
\[
 g^{\mathrm{WH}}(t)=
 \begin{cases}
 C_{+}^{\mathrm{WH}}(t)(D^{\mathrm{WH}})^{-1},&t>0,\\
 C_{+}^{\mathrm{WH}}(-t)^{\mathsf T}(D^{\mathrm{WH}})^{-1},&t<0,\\
 0,&t=0.
 \end{cases}
\]
For \(x>0\), define the finite-dimensional response transform
\[
 \mathcal R^{\mathrm{WH}}(x)
 =\int_0^\infty e^{-xt}C_{+}^{\mathrm{WH}}(t)^{\mathsf T}\,dt
 =(P^{\mathrm{WH}})^{\mathsf T}(xI_r-(L^{\mathrm{WH}})^{\mathsf T})^{-1}O^{\mathsf T}.
\]
Define \((\beta^{\#},A^{\#})\) as the unique pair \((x,M)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb R^{p\times p}\) satisfying
\[
 MO=O(L^{\mathrm{WH}}+xI_r),\qquad
 M[D^{\mathrm{WH}}+\mathcal R^{\mathrm{WH}}(x)]=K^{\mathrm{WH}}.
\]
Equivalently, put
\[
 \Omega^{\mathrm{WH}}
 =\{x>0:\det[D^{\mathrm{WH}}+\mathcal R^{\mathrm{WH}}(x)]\neq0\},
\qquad
 \mathcal A^{\mathrm{WH}}(x)
 =K^{\mathrm{WH}}[D^{\mathrm{WH}}+\mathcal R^{\mathrm{WH}}(x)]^{-1},
\]
let \(\beta^{\#}\) be the unique \(x\in\Omega^{\mathrm{WH}}\) such that
\[
 \mathcal A^{\mathrm{WH}}(x)O=O(L^{\mathrm{WH}}+xI_r),
\]
and set
\[
 A^{\#}=\mathcal A^{\mathrm{WH}}(\beta^{\#}),\qquad
 \mu^{\#}=(I_p-A^{\#}/\beta^{\#})\lambda^{\mathrm{WH}}.
\]
Finally define
\[
 \Phi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH}}(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)
 =(\beta^{\#},A^{\#},\mu^{\#}).
\]
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Ibroad}; \texttt{m}; \texttt{Gamma}; \texttt{Delta}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[def:wiener-hopf-covariance-only-inverse]\label{def:wiener-hopf-covariance-only-inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Defined object.} \texttt{Broad stable covariance-\allowbreak{}only Wiener-\allowbreak{}-\allowbreak{}Hopf inverse}
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Construction.} For \(\gamma=(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\in\mathfrak J_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH}}\), perform the \(r,S,O,R,F,L^{\mathrm{WH}},H^{\mathrm{WH}},P^{\mathrm{WH}},K^{\mathrm{WH}}\), and \(C_{+}^{\mathrm{WH}}\) steps in \(\text{def:wiener-hopf-two-lag-inverse}\) using \((\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\).  Set
\[
 D^{\mathrm{WH},0}=\Delta^{-1}\!\left[
 \Gamma_0-\int_0^\Delta(\Delta-t)
 \{C_{+}^{\mathrm{WH}}(t)+C_{+}^{\mathrm{WH}}(t)^{\mathsf T}\}\,dt
 \right],
\]
\[
 \lambda^{\mathrm{WH},0}=\operatorname{diag}(D^{\mathrm{WH},0}),
 \qquad m^{\mathrm{WH},0}=\Delta\lambda^{\mathrm{WH},0}.
\]
Using this reconstructed mean, evaluate the finite-dimensional response-transform and unique-positive-root steps of \(\Phi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH}}\), and define
\[
 \Psi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH},0}(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)
 =\Phi_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{WH}}(m^{\mathrm{WH},0},\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2).
\]
\par\smallskip\noindent\emph{Inputs.} \texttt{Jbroad}; \texttt{Gamma}; \texttt{Delta}.
\end{definition}

\section{Main results}

\begin{lemma}[lem:covariance-semigroup]\label{lem:covariance-semigroup}
For every law in \(\overline{\mathcal M}_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG}}\), \(Q\succ0\), \(C_{+}(t)=e^{Bt}K\) for \(t>0\), \(K=A(2\beta I_p-A)Q\), \(K-K^{\mathsf T}=AD-DA^{\mathsf T}\), and \(\Gamma_k=T^{k-1}J^2K\) for every \(k\geq1\). Moreover, \(\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1)=\operatorname{im}(K)=\operatorname{im}(A)\) and \(\operatorname{rank}(\Gamma_1)=\operatorname{rank}(A)\), including singular, disconnected, repeated-pole, diamond, and proportional-row cases.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let the law belong to \(\overline{\mathcal M}_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG}}\).  By \(\text{ass:nonnegative-excitation}\), an entry of \(A^d\) is positive only if the support graph contains a directed walk of length \(d\).  If \(d\geq p\), such a walk repeats a vertex and therefore contains a directed cycle.  This contradicts \(\text{ass:acyclic-support}\), under which a self-loop is already a cycle of length one.  Hence
\[
 A^p=0,\qquad \rho(A)=0<\beta,
\tag{1}
\]
where the strict inequality uses \(\text{ass:positive-decay}\).  All remaining membership conditions of \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}}\) are exactly those in \(\text{def:closure-class}\).  Thus the law belongs to \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{stab}}\).

Apply \(\text{lem:stable-covariance-semigroup}\).  It gives, with the same definitions of \(B,T,J,Q,K\),
\[
 Q\succ0,\quad C_+(t)=e^{Bt}K,\quad K=A(2\beta I_p-A)Q,
\]
\[
 K-K^{\mathsf T}=AD-DA^{\mathsf T},\quad
 \Gamma_k=T^{k-1}J^2K,\qquad
 \operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1)=\operatorname{im}(K)=\operatorname{im}(A),
\]
and therefore \(\operatorname{rank}(\Gamma_1)=\operatorname{rank}(A)\).  Equation (1) also shows that every eigenvalue of \(B\) equals \(-\beta\); its algebraic multiplicity, Jordan structure, and the rank of \(A\) were never restricted.  Relabeling into topological order permits arbitrary disconnected blocks, while diamonds and proportional rows merely change the entries and rank of the nilpotent matrix.  The displayed conclusions therefore include singular, disconnected, repeated-pole, diamond, and proportional-row cases.
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} The acyclic covariance lemma is now a direct specialization of the stable common-exponential semigroup and retains its singular-image conclusion. \textit{Gap.} Published complete-lag and full-transform formulas do not state this exact fixed-bin factorization with \(\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1)=\operatorname{im}(A)\) for singular acyclic excitation. \textit{Consumer.} It supplies the observable singular active space for the acyclic inverse.

\begin{theorem}[thm:two-lag-exact-recovery]\label{thm:two-lag-exact-recovery}
For every law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\), the map \(\Phi_{\Delta,r}\) is well defined and basis invariant, \(r\geq1\), \(F\) represents \(T|_S\), \(q=e^{-\beta\Delta}\), \(E^r=0\), and \(\Phi_{\Delta,r}(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)=(\beta,A,\mu)\). In particular, \((m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\) identifies the labeled graph and every amplitude, decay, and baseline exactly even when \(A\) is singular, disconnected, or has repeated poles.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \(\text{lem:covariance-semigroup}\), nonemptiness gives \(r=\operatorname{rank}(A)\geq1\) and \(S=\operatorname{im}(A)=\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1)\).  Since \(BA=AB\), the space \(S\) is invariant under \(B\), and therefore under \(T=e^{B\Delta}\) and \(J\).  For an orthonormal basis \(O\) of \(S\), put \(R=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_1\).  Then \(\Gamma_1=OR\), and \(R\) has row rank \(r\), so \(RR^{\mathsf T}\) is positive definite and invertible.

There is a unique matrix \(F_0\) satisfying \(TO=OF_0\).  The semigroup identity gives \(\Gamma_2=T\Gamma_1=OF_0R\); multiplication on the left by \(O^{\mathsf T}\) and on the right by the right inverse \(R^{\mathsf T}(RR^{\mathsf T})^{-1}\) yields
\[
 F=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_2R^{\mathsf T}(RR^{\mathsf T})^{-1}=F_0.
\]
Thus \(F\) represents \(T|_S\).  If \(A_S=O^{\mathsf T}AO\) denotes the coordinate matrix of \(A|_S\), then \(A_S^r=0\) and
\[
 F=e^{(-\beta I_r+A_S)\Delta}=q_0e^{A_S\Delta},\qquad q_0=e^{-\beta\Delta}\in(0,1).
\]
All eigenvalues of \(e^{A_S\Delta}\) equal one, so \(\operatorname{tr}(F)=rq_0\).  Hence the definition's scalar satisfies \(q=q_0\), and \(\beta^\star=-\Delta^{-1}\log q=\beta\).  Furthermore
\[
 E=q^{-1}F-I_r=e^{A_S\Delta}-I_r
\]
is a polynomial in \(A_S\) with zero constant term.  It is nilpotent and \(E^r=0\).  The mutually inverse exponential and logarithm polynomials on nilpotent matrices give
\[
 \sum_{h=1}^{r-1}\frac{(-1)^{h+1}}hE^h=A_S\Delta,
\]
so \(L^\star=-\beta I_r+A_S\) is exactly the coordinate matrix of \(B|_S\).  Since its only eigenvalue is \(-\beta\), it is invertible.  Since the only eigenvalue of \(F-I_r\) is \(q-1\neq0\), that matrix is also invertible.  On \(S\),
\[
 J_S=(L^\star)^{-1}(F-I_r),\qquad H^\star=L^\star(F-I_r)^{-1}=J_S^{-1},
\]
where commutativity follows because all matrices are polynomials or rational functions of \(A_S\).

The columns of \(K\) lie in \(S\).  Writing \(K=OK_S\), the identity \(\Gamma_1=J^2K\) becomes \(R=J_S^2K_S\).  Therefore
\[
 K^\star=O(H^\star)^2R=OK_S=K.
\]
It follows from \(K-K^{\mathsf T}=AD-DA^{\mathsf T}\) that
\[
 Z_{ij}=A_{ij}\lambda_j-A_{ji}\lambda_i.
\]
Acyclicity forbids a self-loop and forbids the two-cycle consisting of both \(i\to j\) and \(j\to i\).  Nonnegativity and \(\lambda_i>0\) consequently imply, for \(i\neq j\),
\[
 A_{ij}=\frac{\max\{Z_{ij},0\}}{\lambda_j}.
\]
The mean identity gives \(\lambda=m/\Delta\).  Integrating the Hawkes kernel in the stationary mean equation gives \(\lambda=\mu+(A/\beta)\lambda\), and hence \(\mu=(I_p-A/\beta)\lambda\).  Thus every component produced by \(\Phi_{\Delta,r}\) equals the structural component.

Finally, replace \(O\) by another orthonormal basis \(O'=OU\), where \(U\) is orthogonal.  Then \(R'=U^{\mathsf T}R\), \(F'=U^{\mathsf T}FU\), \(E'=U^{\mathsf T}EU\), \(L'=U^{\mathsf T}LU\), and \(H'=U^{\mathsf T}HU\).  Hence \(O'(H')^2R'=OH^2R\).  All later operations are basis free, proving basis invariance.  This argument uses the actual image and actual rank only, so singularity, disconnection, proportional rows, and repeated Jordan poles cause no exception.
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} The theorem isolates a finite sufficient statistic and an explicit inverse for a causal network observed only through coarse counts. \textit{Gap.} Koyama2025 Section 3.4, page 8, Theorem 2 and Equation (18), proves \(F_{\Delta t}^{(cg)}(\nu\Delta t)=F^{(hw)}(\nu)\Delta t+O(\Delta t^3)\) as \(\Delta t\to0\), and Corollary 1 compares that surrogate spectrum with the binned spectrum; Section 3.5, pages 8--9, Equations (19)--(20), defines a quadratic-form estimator. Kirchner2016 Section 3.2, Theorem 2 and Equation (21), proves \(N^{(\Delta)}\Rightarrow N\) only as \(\Delta\to0\), while Section 3.3, Equations (25)--(28), gives parallel INAR(\(\infty\)) and Hawkes lag equations. EtesamiZhangSinghalKiyavash2016 Corollary 4 and Proposition 6 require full spectral zeros and Laplace transforms, and ZhouPapadogeorgou2025 Theorem 3.1 and Proposition 3.1 use the full aggregated-count law; none gives an exact finite-\(\Delta\) inverse for the original labeled process from one mean and two covariance matrices. \textit{Consumer.} QiaoCaiWuXiangZhangHao2023's metropolitan cellular-alarm setting can recover within-bin direction at its native one-to-nine-second resolution without imputing latent event times.

\begin{proposition}[prop:empty-graph-equivalence]\label{prop:empty-graph-equivalence}
Within \(\overline{\mathcal M}_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG}}\), \(\Gamma_1=0\) if and only if \(A=0\). On this stratum \(N_1,\ldots,N_p\) are independent homogeneous Poisson processes with rates \(\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_p\), every \(\beta>0\) induces the same observable law, and \(\beta\) is not identified.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The equivalence \(\Gamma_1=0\Longleftrightarrow A=0\) follows immediately from \(\operatorname{rank}(\Gamma_1)=\operatorname{rank}(A)\) in \(\text{lem:covariance-semigroup}\).  If \(A=0\), then \(\text{ass:hawkes-intensity}\) reduces to \(\ell_i(t)=\mu_i\).  For disjoint labeled intervals, the conditional survivor formula obtained by integrating this deterministic hazard is exponential with rate equal to the sum of the corresponding \(\mu_i\)'s, and the conditional label probability at a jump is \(\mu_i/\sum_j\mu_j\).  Iterating these two conditional laws shows that the superposed process is homogeneous Poisson and its labels are independent identically distributed marks with those probabilities.  Poisson thinning therefore gives independent homogeneous Poisson coordinates of rates \(\mu_i\).  The resulting law contains no occurrence of \(\beta\).  Thus every \(\beta>0\), with the same \(\mu\) and \(A=0\), induces the same observable law, so the decay is not identified on precisely this stratum.
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} The proposition states the exact boundary on which decay recovery must fail and prevents an estimator from fabricating information at the empty graph. \textit{Gap.} The finite-bin papers treat positive-excitation parameterizations or likelihood identifiability and do not state this complete two-lag observational-equivalence class. \textit{Consumer.} The proposition tells a graph-recovery pipeline when it must report a set-valued decay rather than a numerical estimate.

\begin{theorem}[thm:covariance-only-recovery]\label{thm:covariance-only-recovery}
For every law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\), \(\Psi_{\Delta,r}(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)=(\beta,A,\mu)\). Equivalently, after the positive-lag inverse reconstructs \(\widetilde C_{+}(t)=C_{+}(t)\) on \((0,\Delta]\), the formula \(D=\Delta^{-1}[\Gamma_0-\int_0^\Delta(\Delta-t)\{C_{+}(t)+C_{+}(t)^{\mathsf T}\}\,dt]\) recovers \(\lambda=\operatorname{diag}(D)\), so the mean is redundant.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix a law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\).  By \(\text{thm:two-lag-exact-recovery}\), \(r=\operatorname{rank}(\Gamma_1)\geq1\), the positive-lag part of \(\Phi_{\Delta,r}\) is well defined and basis invariant, and it recovers \(q=e^{-\beta\Delta}\).  We verify directly that the positive-lag covariance curve used in \(\text{def:covariance-only-inverse}\) is the true one.

By \(\text{lem:covariance-semigroup}\),
\[
 S=\operatorname{im}(\Gamma_1)=\operatorname{im}(A)=\operatorname{im}(K),
 \qquad \Gamma_1=J^2K,
 \qquad \Gamma_2=T\Gamma_1.
\tag{1}
\]
The space \(S\) is invariant under \(A\), hence under \(B=A-\beta I_p\), \(T=e^{B\Delta}\), and \(J=\int_0^\Delta e^{Bu}\,du\).  Since \(R=O^{\mathsf T}\Gamma_1\) has full row rank and \(\Gamma_1=OR\), (1) and the definition of \(F\) give
\[
 F=O^{\mathsf T}TO.
\tag{2}
\]
Let \(A_S=O^{\mathsf T}AO\).  Acyclicity implies \(A^p=0\), and the restriction \(A|_S:S\to S\) is nilpotent on the \(r\)-dimensional space \(S\), so \(A_S^r=0\).  Equation (2) becomes
\[
 F=e^{-\beta\Delta}e^{A_S\Delta}=q e^{A_S\Delta},
 \qquad E=q^{-1}F-I_r=e^{A_S\Delta}-I_r.
\tag{3}
\]
For a nilpotent matrix, the scalar formal-power-series identity \(\log(e^x)=x\) may be evaluated in the quotient ring \(\mathbb R[x]/(x^r)\); all sums are finite there.  Hence the finite polynomial in \(\text{def:two-lag-inverse}\) satisfies
\[
 \sum_{h=1}^{r-1}\frac{(-1)^{h+1}}{h}E^h=\Delta A_S,
 \qquad L^\star=-\beta I_r+A_S=O^{\mathsf T}BO.
\tag{4}
\]
All eigenvalues of \(L^\star\) equal \(-\beta<0\); therefore \(L^\star\) and \(F-I_r=e^{L^\star\Delta}-I_r\) are invertible.  If \(J_S=O^{\mathsf T}JO\), then invariance of \(S\) and (4) give
\[
 J_S=\int_0^\Delta e^{L^\star u}\,du
 =(L^\star)^{-1}(F-I_r),
 \qquad H^\star=L^\star(F-I_r)^{-1}=J_S^{-1}.
\tag{5}
\]
Here \(F=e^{L^\star\Delta}\), so \(F-I_r\) commutes with \(L^\star\), which justifies the displayed order in the inverse of \(J_S\).
Since the columns of \(K\) lie in \(S\), equations (1) and (5) yield
\[
 R=O^{\mathsf T}J^2K=J_S^2O^{\mathsf T}K,
 \qquad K^\star=O(H^\star)^2R=OO^{\mathsf T}K=K.
\tag{6}
\]
Consequently, for every \(t\in(0,\Delta]\),
\[
 \widetilde C_{+}(t)
 =Oe^{L^\star t}(H^\star)^2R
 =e^{Bt}K
 =C_{+}(t),
\tag{7}
\]
where the last equality is \(\text{lem:covariance-semigroup}\).  Equality (7) also proves that this reconstructed curve is independent of the chosen orthonormal basis \(O\).

Apply \(\text{lem:zero-lag-bin-covariance}\), which is applicable because every acyclic law belongs to the stable class.  Substitution of (7) into its inverse identity gives
\[
 D^\star=\Delta^{-1}\!\left[\Gamma_0-
 \int_0^\Delta(\Delta-t)
 \{\widetilde C_{+}(t)+\widetilde C_{+}(t)^{\mathsf T}\}\,dt\right]
 =D.
\tag{8}
\]
Thus \(\lambda^\star=\operatorname{diag}(D^\star)=\lambda\in(0,\infty)^p\), and the reconstructed mean is \(\Delta\lambda^\star=m\).  Every division and inverse used above is therefore valid, and the integral in (8) is finite by (7) and continuity of \(e^{Bt}K\) on \([0,\Delta]\).

The remaining edge and baseline operations in \(\Psi_{\Delta,r}\) are now exactly those in \(\Phi_{\Delta,r}(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\).  By \(\text{thm:two-lag-exact-recovery}\), they return \((\beta,A,\mu)\).  Hence
\[
 \Psi_{\Delta,r}(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)=(\beta,A,\mu).
\]
This proves both exact recovery and redundancy of the separately supplied mean.
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} The result determines whether the diagonal jump in zero-lag bin covariance carries all missing first-order information. \textit{Gap.} Hawkes1971Spectra and BacryMuzy2016 keep intensity as a separate first-order input, while AchabBacryGaiffasMastromatteoMuzy2017 adds third-order cumulants rather than isolating intensity from \(\Gamma_0\). \textit{Consumer.} Legacy alarm archives that retain covariance summaries but not reliable marginal count totals can still identify the directed Hawkes mechanism.

\begin{lemma}[lem:finite-depth-geometric-mixing]\label{lem:finite-depth-geometric-mixing}
For every fixed \(P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\), the bin-count sequence \(X\) is strongly mixing at a geometric rate. Fix \(p\), \(\Delta\), and \(\kappa\in(0,1/2)\). Uniformly over \(P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)\), the Poisson immigration--birth representation has genealogical depth at most \(p-1\): every child follows a directed edge of \(G(A)\), so a longer lineage would repeat a label and create a directed cycle. Conditional offspring delays have density \(\beta e^{-\beta u}\), \(\beta\geq\underline\beta=-\Delta^{-1}\log(1-\kappa)\), every event has total offspring mean at most \(\overline\nu=\overline a/\underline\beta\), and the total immigrant rate is at most \(\overline\mu\). Consequently the cluster duration has the uniform tail bound \(P_{\theta}(D_{\mathrm{cl}}>u)\leq C_{\mathrm{mix}}e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}u}\), where \(c_{\mathrm{mix}}=\underline\beta/2\) and \(C_{\mathrm{mix}}=1+\{1+2\overline\mu c_{\mathrm{mix}}^{-1}e^{c_{\mathrm{mix}}\Delta}\}\sum_{d=1}^{p-1}(2\overline a/\underline\beta)^d\). Coupling the restrictions of the immigrant Poisson measure on the two sides of a bin gap then gives \(\alpha_{X,\theta}(h)\leq C_{\mathrm{mix}}e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}h\Delta}\) for every integer \(h\geq1\), and \(\alpha_{Y,\theta}(h)\leq C_{\mathrm{mix}}e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}(h-2)_{+}\Delta}\) for every integer \(h\geq1\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Acyclicity implies that a genealogical line uses at most \(p-1\) edges: each child follows an edge of \(G(A)\), and a line of length at least \(p\) repeats a label and contains a directed cycle.  For a parent of label \(j\), the total number of children is Poisson with mean \(\sum_iA_{ij}/\beta\).  The parameter envelope and the column norm inequality give
\[
 \sum_iA_{ij}\leq\sqrt p\|Ae_j\|_2\leq\sqrt p\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\overline a,
\]
while the decay margin gives \(\beta\geq\underline\beta\).  Thus every total offspring mean is at most \(\overline\nu=\overline a/\underline\beta\).  Conditional on a child occurring, its delay has density \(\beta e^{-\beta u}\).  Also \(\sum_i\mu_i\leq\sqrt p\|\mu\|_2\leq\overline\mu\).

Let \(c=c_{\mathrm{mix}}=\underline\beta/2\).  A descendant at generation \(d\) has a path delay equal in law, conditional on its path, to a sum \(S_d\) of \(d\) independent exponential variables of rate \(\beta\).  Exponential Markov inequality gives
\[
 P(S_d>u)\leq e^{-cu}\left(\frac{\beta}{\beta-c}\right)^d\leq2^de^{-cu}.
\]
The expected number of generation-\(d\) descendants is at most \(\overline\nu^d\).  A union bound through expected descendant counts therefore yields
\[
 P(D_{\mathrm{cl}}>u)\leq e^{-cu}\sum_{d=1}^{p-1}(2\overline\nu)^d
 \leq C_{\mathrm{mix}}e^{-cu}.
\]

For the strong-mixing bound, couple the past bins and future bins by using independent copies of every immigrant cluster that intersects both sides of the gap.  If the past ends at time \(\Delta\) and the future begins at \(h\Delta\), such a cluster must have immigrant time \(s\leq\Delta\) and duration exceeding \(h\Delta-s\).  The Poisson union bound and the preceding sharper tail with constant \(C_0=\sum_{d=1}^{p-1}(2\overline a/\underline\beta)^d\) give
\[
 P(\text{a crossing cluster})
 \leq\overline\mu\int_{-\infty}^{\Delta}C_0e^{-c(h\Delta-s)}ds
 =\overline\mu c^{-1}e^{c\Delta}C_0e^{-ch\Delta}
 \leq C_{\mathrm{mix}}e^{-ch\Delta}.
\]
On the complement, the two sigma-fields are functions of disjoint restrictions of the immigrant Poisson measure and independent offspring marks.  The coupling inequality thus proves \(\alpha_{X,\theta}(h)\leq C_{\mathrm{mix}}e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}h\Delta}\).  The process \(Y_k\) depends only on \(X_k,X_{k+1},X_{k+2}\), so its two sigma-fields have an \(X\)-bin gap of \((h-2)_+\), yielding the stated bound for \(\alpha_{Y,\theta}(h)\).  With parameters fixed, the same calculation uses finite law-specific constants and proves geometric mixing on the whole nonempty class.
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} This lemma turns feed-forward structure and bounded primitive parameters into the uniform dependence rate required by the HAC argument. \textit{Gap.} BolyCheyssonNguyen2024 provides compatible general multivariate Hawkes mixing background but does not state this geometric rate from finite genealogical depth; the rate and constants here come from the acyclic common-exponential cluster construction. \textit{Consumer.} The raw-moment central limit theorem can invoke a derived uniform geometric rate instead of assuming one as a separate model restriction.

\begin{lemma}[lem:uniform-bin-eighth-moment]\label{lem:uniform-bin-eighth-moment}
Every fixed law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\) has finite bin-count moments of every order. Fix \(p\), \(\Delta\), and \(\kappa\in(0,1/2)\). Uniformly over \(P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)\), the total progeny of any immigrant cluster is dominated by a depth-\(p-1\) Poisson branching tree whose moment-generating functions obey \(g_0(z)=e^z\) and \(g_d(z)=\exp[z+\overline\nu\{g_{d-1}(z)-1\}]\). For every integer \(u\in\{1,\ldots,16\}\), differentiating the stationary Poisson-cluster generating functional and using the duration bound in \(\text{lem:finite-depth-geometric-mixing}\) gives \[\operatorname{cum}_u(\lVert X_0\rVert_1)\leq\overline\mu\left[\Delta g_{p-1}^{(u)}(0)+2c_{\mathrm{mix}}^{-1}\{C_{\mathrm{mix}}g_{p-1}^{(2u)}(0)\}^{1/2}\right].\] The complete moment--cumulant formulas through order sixteen therefore yield the named finite constant \(M_8=M_8(p,\Delta,\kappa)\), the bound \(\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}\mathbb E_{\theta}\lVert X_0\rVert_2^8\leq M_8\), and \(\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}\mathbb E_{\theta}\lVert X_0\rVert_2^{16}<\infty\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write \(C_d\) for the total progeny of an actual cluster with at most \(d\) generations remaining, counting its root.  By \(\text{lem:finite-depth-geometric-mixing}\), every child follows a directed edge of \(G(A)\).  Hence \(\text{ass:acyclic-support}\) implies that a lineage has at most \(p-1\) edges.  The integrated number of children of an event of type \(j\) is Poisson with mean \(\sum_i A_{ij}/\beta\).  On \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)\), \(\text{ass:decay-margin}\) gives \(\beta\geq\underline\beta\), while \(\text{ass:parameter-envelope}\) gives \(\sum_iA_{ij}\leq\sqrt p\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\overline a\).  Thus this offspring mean is at most \(\overline\nu=\overline a/\underline\beta\).  Independence of the Poisson offspring processes and monotone coupling with enlarged Poisson processes construct dominating progeny variables \(T_d\) such that \(C_d\preceq T_d\) and
\[
 T_0=1,
 \qquad
 T_d\mathrel{\stackrel{d}{=}} 1+\sum_{a=1}^{P_{\overline\nu}}T_{d-1,a},
 \qquad 1\leq d\leq p-1,
\tag{1}
\]
where \(P_{\overline\nu}\) is Poisson with mean \(\overline\nu\), the variables \(T_{d-1,a}\) are independent copies of the dominating depth-\(d-1\) tree, and they are independent of \(P_{\overline\nu}\).  Conditional expectation in (1) proves inductively, for every \(z\geq0\),
\[
 \mathbb E e^{zT_d}
 = \exp\!\left[z+\overline\nu\{g_{d-1}(z)-1\}\right]
 =g_d(z),
 \qquad g_0(z)=e^z.
\tag{2}
\]
Each \(g_d\) is an iterated exponential and is finite on a neighborhood of zero.  Differentiating (2) at zero yields
\[
 \mathbb E T_d^u\leq g_d^{(u)}(0)<\infty
 \quad\text{for every fixed integer }u\geq1.
\tag{3}
\]

Let \(Z_s\) be the total number of points in \([0,\Delta]\) belonging to a cluster whose immigrant occurs at time \(s\).  The immigrant processes of the \(p\) types are independent Poisson processes.  Partitioning the immigrant-time axis into bounded intervals, using additivity of cumulants for independent compound-Poisson sums, and then applying monotone convergence gives, for the total bin count \(H=\lVert X_0\rVert_1\),
\[
 \operatorname{cum}_u(H)
 =\sum_{j=1}^p\mu_j\int_{-\infty}^{\Delta}
       \mathbb E_j(Z_s^u)\,ds.
\tag{4}
\]
This is a derivation of the Poisson-cluster cumulant identity: on a bounded immigrant interval the logarithm of the compound-Poisson moment-generating function is the immigrant intensity times \(\mathbb E(e^{zZ_s}-1)\), whose \(u\)-th derivative at zero is \(\mathbb E Z_s^u\); the nonnegative integrands permit the limit to the whole half-line.  In particular, every cumulant in (4) is nonnegative.

Immigrants with \(s>\Delta\) contribute nothing.  If \(0\leq s\leq\Delta\), then \(Z_s\leq T_{p-1}\).  If \(s<0\), the event \(Z_s>0\) implies \(D_{\mathrm{cl}}>-s\).  Therefore Cauchy--Schwarz, (3), and the duration bound in \(\text{lem:finite-depth-geometric-mixing}\) give, for every integer \(u\geq1\),
\[
 \mathbb E_j Z_s^u
 \leq
 \left\{\mathbb E T_{p-1}^{2u}
 P_{\theta}(D_{\mathrm{cl}}>-s)\right\}^{1/2}
 \leq
 \{C_{\mathrm{mix}}g_{p-1}^{(2u)}(0)\}^{1/2}
 e^{c_{\mathrm{mix}}s/2}.
\tag{5}
\]
Moreover \(\sum_j\mu_j\leq\sqrt p\lVert\mu\rVert_2\leq\overline\mu\) by \(\text{ass:parameter-envelope}\).  Substitution of (3)--(5) into (4), followed by integration over \(( -\infty,0)\) and \([0,\Delta]\), proves for every \(1\leq u\leq16\),
\[
 \operatorname{cum}_u(H)
 \leq\overline\mu\left[
 \Delta g_{p-1}^{(u)}(0)
 +2c_{\mathrm{mix}}^{-1}
 \{C_{\mathrm{mix}}g_{p-1}^{(2u)}(0)\}^{1/2}
 \right].
\tag{6}
\]
At \(u=16\), the tail step in (5) uses exactly \(g_{p-1}^{(32)}(0)\).

For a random variable with finite moments, its \(v\)-th moment is the complete Bell polynomial \(B_v\) in its first \(v\) cumulants.  Here this identity follows directly by differentiating \(\exp\{\sum_{u\geq1}\operatorname{cum}_u(H)z^u/u!\}\) through order \(v\).  Every coefficient of \(B_v\) is a nonnegative integer, and the cumulants in (4) are nonnegative.  Applying (6) in \(B_8\) gives the declared finite constant \(M_8(p,\Delta,\kappa)\).  Applying it in the complete Bell polynomial \(B_{16}\) gives a finite bound depending only on \(p,\Delta,\kappa\) for \(\mathbb E H^{16}\).  Since \(\lVert X_0\rVert_2\leq H\),
\[
 \sup_{\theta:P_\theta\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}
 \mathbb E_\theta\lVert X_0\rVert_2^8\leq M_8,
 \qquad
 \sup_{\theta:P_\theta\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}
 \mathbb E_\theta\lVert X_0\rVert_2^{16}<\infty.
\tag{7}
\]
For a fixed law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\), the same argument uses its finite offspring means, positive decay, finite immigrant rate, and the law-dependent geometric duration bound supplied by \(\text{lem:finite-depth-geometric-mixing}\).  Since (3) holds at every fixed order, (4)--(6) then give every fixed bin-count moment.
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} The recursive cluster bound supplies eighth moments of the quadratic raw-moment process needed for explicit four-point cumulant summability and uniform long-run covariance estimation. \textit{Gap.} CheyssonLang2022 assumes moment regularity for its spectral limit theory; it does not derive a uniform sixteenth-bin-count envelope from bounded acyclic Hawkes primitives. \textit{Consumer.} Uniform HAC consistency and Gaussian calibration no longer depend on an unexplained independent moment assumption.

\begin{lemma}[lem:bin-moment-limit]\label{lem:bin-moment-limit}
For every fixed law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\), \(\widehat\xi_n\to\xi\) and \(\widehat\eta_n=\mathcal C(\widehat\xi_n)\to\eta=(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\) in probability. Fix \(p\), \(\Delta\), and \(\kappa\in(0,1/2)\). The derived geometric-mixing and sixteenth-bin-count bounds imply that \(\Omega(\theta)\) is finite uniformly over \(P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)\) and \[\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}d_{\mathrm{BL}}\left(\mathcal L_{\theta}\{\sqrt n(\widehat\xi_n-\xi(\theta))\},\mathcal N\{0,\Omega(\theta)\}\right)\longrightarrow0.\] Because \(\widehat\eta_n=\mathcal C(\widehat\xi_n)\) and \(\eta=\mathcal C(\xi)=(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\), the same uniform statement holds for \(\sqrt n(\widehat\eta_n-\eta(\theta))\) with covariance \(D\mathcal C_{\xi(\theta)}\Omega(\theta)D\mathcal C_{\xi(\theta)}^{\mathsf T}\). If \(b_n\to\infty\) and \(b_n=o(n^{1/2})\), then for every \(\varepsilon>0\), \[\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}P_{\theta}\{\lVert\widehat\Omega_n-\Omega(\theta)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}>\varepsilon\}\longrightarrow0.\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put \(d_0=p+2p^2\), \(N_n=n-2\), and \(U_k=Y_k-\xi\).  Constants below may depend on \(p,\Delta,\kappa\), but not on \(\theta\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)\), on time indices, or on coordinate indices.  The strengthened \(\text{lem:uniform-bin-eighth-moment}\) gives
\[
 \sup_\theta\mathbb E_\theta\lVert X_0\rVert_2^{16}<\infty.
\tag{1}
\]
Every coordinate of \(Y_0\) is either a coordinate of \(X_0\) or a product of one coordinate of \(X_0\) and one coordinate of \(X_1\) or \(X_2\).  Cauchy--Schwarz and stationarity therefore turn (1) into
\[
 \sup_\theta\mathbb E_\theta\lVert Y_0\rVert_2^8<\infty,
 \qquad
 \sup_\theta\mathbb E_\theta\lVert U_0\rVert_2^8<\infty.
\tag{2}
\]
The fixed-law versions follow from the all-orders part of that lemma.  By \(\text{lem:finite-depth-geometric-mixing}\), after increasing a constant to cover lags zero, one may fix \(\rho=e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}\Delta}\in(0,1)\) such that
\[
 \alpha_{Y,\theta}(h)\leq C_0\rho^h,
 \qquad h\geq0,
\tag{3}
\]
uniformly on the margin class.  A fixed law has the analogous bound with law-dependent \(C_0\) and \(\rho\).

We first prove the product inequality needed for every covariance and fourth-cumulant estimate below.  Let \(V\) and \(W\) be measurable with respect to two sigma-fields whose strong-mixing coefficient is at most \(a\), and suppose \(V\in L^s\), \(W\in L^t\), where \(s,t>1\) and \(s^{-1}+t^{-1}<1\).  Then
\[
 |\operatorname{Cov}(V,W)|
 \leq C_{s,t}\lVert V\rVert_s\lVert W\rVert_t
 a^{1-s^{-1}-t^{-1}}.
\tag{4}
\]
To derive (4), first normalize both norms to one and split each variable into its positive and negative parts.  For nonnegative \(V,W\), layer cake and Fubini give
\[
 \operatorname{Cov}(V,W)=
 \int_0^\infty\!\int_0^\infty
 \{P(V>x,W>y)-P(V>x)P(W>y)\}\,dx\,dy.
\tag{5}
\]
The absolute value of the integrand is bounded simultaneously by \(a\), \(2P(V>x)\), and \(2P(W>y)\).  Markov's inequality consequently bounds the absolute value of (5) by
\[
 \int_0^\infty\!\int_0^\infty
 \min\{a,2x^{-s},2y^{-t}\}\,dx\,dy.
\tag{6}
\]
For \(a>0\), set \(A=(2/a)^{1/s}\) and \(B=(2/a)^{1/t}\).  On \([0,A]\times[0,B]\), (6) is at most \(aAB\).  On \((A,\infty)\times[0,B]\) it is at most \(2B\int_A^\infty x^{-s}dx\), and the symmetric rectangle has the analogous bound.  Each is a constant times \(a^{1-s^{-1}-t^{-1}}\).  On the last rectangle, the changes of variables \(x=Au\), \(y=Bv\) leave this same power of \(a\) times
\[
 \int_1^\infty\!\int_1^\infty\min\{u^{-s},v^{-t}\}\,du\,dv<\infty;
\tag{7}
\]
splitting at \(v=u^{s/t}\) proves finiteness precisely because \(s^{-1}+t^{-1}<1\).  Summing the four sign combinations proves (4).  If \(a=0\), the integrand in (5) vanishes and (4) follows by continuity.  This is an elementary proof of (4), not an additional mixing assumption.

Taking \(s=t=4\) in (4), and using (2)--(3), gives for every pair of scalar coordinates
\[
 |\operatorname{Cov}_\theta(U_0^a,U_h^b)|
 \leq C\rho^{|h|/2}.
\tag{8}
\]
Thus all coordinate covariance sequences are absolutely summable, uniformly.  Since \(d_0\) is fixed,
\[
 \sup_\theta\sum_{h\in\mathbb Z}
 \lVert\operatorname{Cov}_\theta(Y_0,Y_h)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty.
\tag{9}
\]
In particular \(\Omega(\theta)\) exists and is uniformly finite.

We next supply the four-point calculation.  Fix ordered integers \(s_1\leq s_2\leq s_3\leq s_4\), scalar coordinates \(a_1,\ldots,a_4\), and put \(V_\ell=U_{s_\ell}^{a_\ell}\).  Let
\[
 G=\max_{1\leq j\leq3}(s_{j+1}-s_j),
\]
and choose \(j\) attaining the maximum.  For the left and right products, generalized H\"older and (2) give
\[
 \left\lVert\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}V_\ell\right\rVert_{8/j}\leq C,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert\prod_{\ell=j+1}^{4}V_\ell\right\rVert_{8/(4-j)}\leq C.
\tag{10}
\]
The two reciprocal exponents in (10) sum to \(1/2\).  Applying (4) across the gap \(G\) therefore gives
\[
 \left|\operatorname{Cov}\left(
 \prod_{\ell=1}^{j}V_\ell,
 \prod_{\ell=j+1}^{4}V_\ell\right)\right|
 \leq C\rho^{G/2}.
\tag{11}
\]
Here and below, coincident times, for which \(G=0\), are covered directly by (2).

All \(V_\ell\) are centered, so
\[
 \operatorname{cum}(V_1,V_2,V_3,V_4)
 =\mathbb E(V_1V_2V_3V_4)
 -\gamma_{12}\gamma_{34}-\gamma_{13}\gamma_{24}-\gamma_{14}\gamma_{23},
 \qquad \gamma_{ab}=\mathbb E(V_aV_b).
\tag{12}
\]
If \(j=1\), the first term in (12) is \(\operatorname{Cov}(V_1,V_2V_3V_4)\), which is controlled by (11); in each of the three pairing terms, the covariance containing \(V_1\) crosses the gap \(G=s_2-s_1\), so (8) controls that factor and (2) bounds the other.  If \(j=3\), the same cancellation holds with \(V_4\) separated from \(V_1V_2V_3\).  If \(j=2\), subtracting \(\gamma_{12}\gamma_{34}\) turns the first two terms of (12) into \(\operatorname{Cov}(V_1V_2,V_3V_4)\), controlled by (11), while every covariance in each of \(\gamma_{13}\gamma_{24}\) and \(\gamma_{14}\gamma_{23}\) crosses \(s_3-s_2=G\).  Thus the \(1|3\), \(2|2\), and \(3|1\) cases all yield the uniform envelope
\[
 |\operatorname{cum}(U_{s_1}^{a_1},U_{s_2}^{a_2},U_{s_3}^{a_3},U_{s_4}^{a_4})|
 \leq C\rho^{G/2}.
\tag{13}
\]

Let \(I\) be an integer interval of length \(\ell\), let \(a\in\mathbb R^{d_0}\) satisfy \(\lVert a\rVert_2=1\), and set \(S_I=\sum_{k\in I}a^{\mathsf T}U_k\).  Equation (9) gives \(\operatorname{Var}(S_I)\leq C\ell\).  By multilinearity of cumulants and (13), ordering four indices and writing their three adjacent gaps as \(d_1,d_2,d_3\) gives
\[
 |\operatorname{cum}_4(S_I)|
 \leq 4!C\ell
 \sum_{d_1,d_2,d_3\geq0}\rho^{\max(d_1,d_2,d_3)/2}
 \leq C'\ell.
\tag{14}
\]
The series is finite because the number of triples with maximum \(q\) is \((q+1)^3-q^3=O((q+1)^2)\).  The centered fourth-moment identity, obtained by expanding the fourth derivative of the cumulant generating function, is \(\mathbb E S_I^4=\operatorname{cum}_4(S_I)+3\operatorname{Var}(S_I)^2\).  Hence
\[
 \sup_\theta\mathbb E_\theta
 \left|\sum_{k\in I}a^{\mathsf T}U_k\right|^4
 \leq C''\ell^2.
\tag{15}
\]

Equation (9) also gives
\[
 \sup_\theta\mathbb E_\theta
 \left\lVert N_n^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{N_n-1}U_k\right\rVert_2^2
 \leq C N_n^{-1}.
\tag{16}
\]
Therefore \(\widehat\xi_n\to\xi\) in probability uniformly on the margin class.  The same calculation with law-dependent constants gives convergence for each fixed law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\).  Since \(\mathcal C\) in \(\text{def:centering-map}\) is a quadratic polynomial and hence continuous, \(\widehat\eta_n=\mathcal C(\widehat\xi_n)\to\mathcal C(\xi)=\eta=(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\), proving the two asserted consistency claims.

We now prove the uniform Gaussian approximation.  For a unit vector \(a\), put \(Z_k=a^{\mathsf T}U_k\), choose \(\ell_n=\lfloor N_n^{1/2}\rfloor\) and \(g_n=\lceil c_0\log N_n\rceil\) with \(c_0>(2c_{\mathrm{mix}}\Delta)^{-1}\), and partition the observations into consecutive large blocks of length \(\ell_n\), separating gaps of length \(g_n\), plus a terminal remainder.  There are \(v_n\leq N_n/\ell_n\) large blocks.  The discarded set has size \(O(N_ng_n/\ell_n+\ell_n)\), so (9) makes its normalized sum converge to zero in \(L^2\), uniformly.  Iterating the defining strong-mixing event inequality, first for simple functions and then by bounded approximation, shows that replacing the large-block characteristic functions by those of independent block copies changes their product by at most
\[
 4v_n\alpha_{Y,\theta}(g_n)
 \leq C N_n^{1/2}e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}\Delta g_n}=o(1)
\tag{17}
\]
uniformly.

Let \(B_{b,n}\) denote the independent copies of the centered large-block sums.  From (15) and (9),
\[
 \sum_{b=1}^{v_n}\frac{\mathbb E|B_{b,n}|^4}{N_n^2}
 \leq C\frac{v_n\ell_n^2}{N_n^2}
 =O(\ell_n/N_n)=o(1),
\tag{18}
\]
and Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\[
 \sum_{b=1}^{v_n}\frac{\mathbb E|B_{b,n}|^3}{N_n^{3/2}}
 \leq C\frac{v_n\ell_n^{3/2}}{N_n^{3/2}}
 =O\{(\ell_n/N_n)^{1/2}\}=o(1).
\tag{19}
\]
For fixed \(u\in\mathbb R\), the elementary bound \(|e^{ix}-1-ix+x^2/2|\leq |x|^3/6\), (19), and \(\max_b\operatorname{Var}(B_{b,n})/N_n\leq C\ell_n/N_n=o(1)\) show, after taking logarithms of the product, that
\[
 \prod_{b=1}^{v_n}\mathbb E\exp(iuB_{b,n}/\sqrt{N_n})
 =\exp\{-u^2\sigma_{a,n}^2/2\}+o(1),
 \quad
 \sigma_{a,n}^2=N_n^{-1}\sum_{b=1}^{v_n}\operatorname{Var}(B_{b,n}),
\tag{20}
\]
uniformly in \(\theta\).  If \(\gamma_a(h)=\operatorname{Cov}(Z_0,Z_h)\), stationarity gives
\[
 \ell_n^{-1}\operatorname{Var}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\ell_n}Z_k\right)
 =\sum_{|h|<\ell_n}(1-|h|/\ell_n)\gamma_a(h).
\tag{21}
\]
The geometric envelope (8), together with \(v_n\ell_n/N_n\to1\), makes (21) converge uniformly to \(a^{\mathsf T}\Omega(\theta)a\).  Combining (17), (20), (21), and the negligible discarded observations proves the scalar characteristic-function approximation uniformly for every fixed \(a\).

To obtain the stated vector bounded-Lipschitz conclusion without imposing compactness on the parameter set, argue by contradiction.  If it failed, choose \(\theta_n\) along which the discrepancy is bounded away from zero.  The uniformly bounded positive-semidefinite matrices \(\Omega(\theta_n)\) have a convergent subsequence, say to \(\Omega_*\).  The preceding scalar calculation applied to \(a=u/\lVert u\rVert_2\) for each \(u\in\mathbb R^{d_0}\) gives pointwise convergence of the vector characteristic functions to \(\exp(-u^{\mathsf T}\Omega_*u/2)\).  Equation (16) gives uniform tightness.  On a sufficiently large ball, the unit bounded-Lipschitz class has a finite uniform sup-norm net; continuous compactly supported approximations to the finitely many net members are approximated uniformly by functions with integrable Fourier transforms, for which characteristic-function convergence implies convergence of expectations.  Outside the ball, (16) controls both the empirical laws and the Gaussian laws.  Hence the bounded-Lipschitz discrepancy along the subsequence tends to zero, a contradiction.  Thus
\[
 \sup_\theta d_{\mathrm{BL}}\left(
 \mathcal L_\theta\{\sqrt n(\widehat\xi_n-\xi(\theta))\},
 \mathcal N\{0,\Omega(\theta)\}\right)\longrightarrow0,
\tag{22}
\]
where \(N_n/n\to1\) justifies replacing \(N_n\) by \(n\).

For \(h=\widehat\xi_n-\xi\), the exact quadratic expansion of \(\mathcal C\) is
\[
 \mathcal C(\xi+h)-\mathcal C(\xi)
 =D\mathcal C_\xi h+R(h),
 \qquad \lVert R(h)\rVert_2\leq C\lVert h\rVert_2^2.
\tag{23}
\]
The population first and second raw moments, and hence \(D\mathcal C_\xi\), are uniformly bounded by (1).  Equation (16) implies \(\sqrt nR(h)=o_{P_\theta}(1)\) uniformly.  Applying the uniformly bounded linear maps \(D\mathcal C_{\xi(\theta)}\) to (22), and then using (23), proves the asserted uniform Gaussian approximation for \(\sqrt n(\widehat\eta_n-\eta)\) with covariance \(D\mathcal C_\xi\Omega D\mathcal C_\xi^{\mathsf T}\).

It remains to prove the HAC claim.  For coordinates \(a,b,c,d\) and arbitrary integers \(h,h'\geq0\), the centered fourth-cumulant identity gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 &\operatorname{Cov}(U_0^aU_h^b,U_t^cU_{t+h'}^d)\\
 &\quad=\operatorname{cum}(U_0^a,U_h^b,U_t^c,U_{t+h'}^d)
 +\operatorname{Cov}(U_0^a,U_t^c)\operatorname{Cov}(U_h^b,U_{t+h'}^d)\\
 &\qquad\quad
 +\operatorname{Cov}(U_0^a,U_{t+h'}^d)\operatorname{Cov}(U_h^b,U_t^c).
 \end{aligned}
\tag{24}
\]
Order the multiset \(\{0,h,t,t+h'\}\), and let \(G_t\) be its largest adjacent gap.  Equation (13) bounds the cumulant in (24) by \(C\rho^{G_t/2}\).  For every integer \(q\geq0\), if \(G_t\leq q\), the diameter of these four ordered points is at most \(3q\); because one point is zero, this forces \(|t|\leq3q\).  Thus at most \(6q+1\) integers \(t\) have \(G_t\leq q\), uniformly in \(h,h'\), and consequently
\[
 \sup_{h,h'\geq0}\sum_{t\in\mathbb Z}\rho^{G_t/2}
 \leq 1+\sum_{q=1}^\infty(6q+1)\rho^{(q-1)/2}<\infty.
\tag{25}
\]
For each of the other two terms in (24), (8) gives a convolution of one absolutely summable covariance sequence with a uniformly bounded one.  For example,
\[
 \sum_{t\in\mathbb Z}
 |\operatorname{Cov}(U_0^a,U_t^c)
   \operatorname{Cov}(U_h^b,U_{t+h'}^d)|
 \leq
 \lVert\gamma_{ac}\rVert_1\lVert\gamma_{bd}\rVert_\infty\leq C.
\tag{26}
\]
Equations (24)--(26) prove the required translation-uniform product bound
\[
 \sup_{h,h'\geq0}\sum_{t\in\mathbb Z}
 |\operatorname{Cov}(U_0^aU_h^b,U_t^cU_{t+h'}^d)|<\infty.
\tag{27}
\]

Define the true-mean lag average
\[
 \widetilde\Xi_h=N_n^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{N_n-h-1}U_kU_{k+h}^{\mathsf T}.
\]
For all sufficiently large \(n\), \(b_n<N_n\).  Expanding the two finite averages and setting \(t=\ell-k\), stationarity and (27) yield, uniformly for \(0\leq h,h'\leq b_n\),
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 &\left|\operatorname{Cov}\{(\widetilde\Xi_h)_{ab},
                         (\widetilde\Xi_{h'})_{cd}\}\right|\\
 &\quad\leq N_n^{-2}\sum_{k=0}^{N_n-h-1}
       \sum_{\ell=0}^{N_n-h'-1}
       |\operatorname{Cov}(U_k^aU_{k+h}^b,U_\ell^cU_{\ell+h'}^d)|\\
 &\quad\leq N_n^{-2}\sum_{k=0}^{N_n-h-1}
       \sum_{t\in\mathbb Z}
       |\operatorname{Cov}(U_0^aU_h^b,U_t^cU_{t+h'}^d)|
 \leq C/N_n.
 \end{aligned}
\tag{28}
\]
There are only \(d_0^2\) matrix coordinates, the Bartlett weights lie in \([0,1]\), and symmetrization changes only a fixed factor.  Summing (28) over the \((b_n+1)^2\) lag pairs gives
\[
 \sup_\theta\mathbb E_\theta\left\lVert
 \widetilde\Xi_0-\mathbb E\widetilde\Xi_0
 +\sum_{h=1}^{b_n}w(h/b_n)
 \{\widetilde\Xi_h+\widetilde\Xi_h^{\mathsf T}
   -\mathbb E(\widetilde\Xi_h+\widetilde\Xi_h^{\mathsf T})\}
 \right\rVert_{\mathrm F}^2
 \leq Cb_n^2/N_n=o(1).
\tag{29}
\]

Let \(\Xi_h=\operatorname{Cov}(Y_0,Y_h)\).  Directly from stationarity,
\[
 \mathbb E\widetilde\Xi_h=(1-h/N_n)\Xi_h.
\tag{30}
\]
The geometric envelope (8) is a uniform summable envelope for \(\Xi_h\).  Since \(w(h/b_n)\to1\) for every fixed \(h\), equations (9) and (30), split at a fixed lag and then with that lag sent to infinity, show uniformly that the expectation of the symmetrized expression in (29) converges to
\[
 \Xi_0+\sum_{h=1}^\infty(\Xi_h+\Xi_h^{\mathsf T})
 =\sum_{h\in\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Cov}(Y_0,Y_h)=\Omega(\theta).
\tag{31}
\]

Finally put \(\overline U_n=N_n^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{N_n-1}U_k=\widehat\xi_n-\xi\), and define
\[
 A_{h,n}=N_n^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{N_n-h-1}U_k,
 \qquad
 B_{h,n}=N_n^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{N_n-h-1}U_{k+h}.
\]
The empirical lag matrix in \(\text{def:hac-estimator}\) satisfies the exact expansion
\[
 \widehat\Xi_h-\widetilde\Xi_h
 =-A_{h,n}\overline U_n^{\mathsf T}
  -\overline U_nB_{h,n}^{\mathsf T}
  +(1-h/N_n)\overline U_n\overline U_n^{\mathsf T}.
\tag{32}
\]
By (16), \(\lVert\overline U_n\rVert_2=O_{P_\theta}(N_n^{-1/2})\) uniformly.  The first moment bound from (2) and Markov's inequality give
\[
 \sum_{h=0}^{b_n}\{\lVert A_{h,n}\rVert_2+\lVert B_{h,n}\rVert_2\}=O_{P_\theta}(b_n)
\tag{33}
\]
uniformly.  Therefore (32), after Bartlett weighting and symmetrization, changes the complete lag-window sum by
\[
 O_{P_\theta}\{b_nN_n^{-1/2}+b_nN_n^{-1}\}=o_{P_\theta}(1)
\tag{34}
\]
uniformly, using \(\text{ass:hac-bandwidth}\).  The empty-sum convention covers the finitely many samples before \(b_n<N_n\).  Combining (29), (31), and (34), then using \(\lVert M\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lVert M\rVert_{\mathrm F}\), proves for every \(\varepsilon>0\),
\[
 \sup_{\theta:P_\theta\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}
 P_\theta\{\lVert\widehat\Omega_n-\Omega(\theta)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}>\varepsilon\}
 \longrightarrow0.
\]
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} This lemma now supplies the complete raw and centered moment experiment used by every downstream estimator: pointwise consistency, a uniform bounded-Lipschitz Gaussian approximation, and operator-norm consistency of the corrected Bartlett estimator. \textit{Gap.} The proof derives the needed vector limit and HAC variance bound directly from the finite-depth mixing envelope and Poisson-cluster moments in the inverse's exact raw-lag coordinates; no generic fixed-bin Whittle theorem supplies this joint result. \textit{Consumer.} Rank selection, structural inversion, signed-edge calibration, and every conservative graph interval use this common moment law.

\begin{theorem}[thm:rank-adaptive-estimation]\label{thm:rank-adaptive-estimation}
For every fixed law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\), if \(t_n\downarrow0\), \(\sqrt n\,t_n\to\infty\), \(a_n\downarrow0\), and \(\sqrt n\,a_n\to\infty\), then \(\mathbb P(\widehat r_n=r)\to1\), \(\widehat\theta_n\to(\beta,A,\mu)\) in probability, and \(\mathbb P(\widehat G_n=G(A))\to1\). Fix \(p\), \(\Delta\), and \(\kappa\in(0,1/2)\). On \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)\), under the long-run-covariance nonsingularity and bandwidth conditions, rank selection is uniformly consistent and the structural estimator is uniformly root-\(n\) tight: \[\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}P_{\theta}(\widehat r_n\neq r(\theta))\longrightarrow0,\] \[\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}P_{\theta}\!\left[\sqrt n\{ |\widehat\beta_n-\beta|+\lVert\widehat A_n-A\rVert_{\mathrm F}+\lVert\widehat\mu_n-\mu\rVert_2\}>M\right]=0.\] For every \(\varepsilon>0\), \[\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}P_{\theta}\{\lVert\widehat\Omega_n-\Omega(\theta)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}>\varepsilon\}\longrightarrow0.\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
We separate the fixed-law and uniform arguments.  Under a fixed law in \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}^{\mathrm{DAG},+}\), the fixed-law moment and geometric-mixing conclusions of \(\text{lem:uniform-bin-eighth-moment}\) and \(\text{lem:finite-depth-geometric-mixing}\), together with the clipping calculation (2)--(3) in \(\text{lem:bin-moment-limit}\), imply
\[
 \sqrt n(\widehat\xi_n-\xi)=O_P(1),
 \qquad
 \sqrt n(\widehat\eta_n-\eta)=O_P(1),
\tag{1}
\]
the second relation following from the quadratic centering map in \(\text{def:centering-map}\).

Let \(r=\operatorname{rank}(\Gamma_1)\).  By \(\text{thm:two-lag-exact-recovery}\) and \(A\neq0\) in \(\text{def:model-class}\), \(r\geq1\).  The singular-value min--max formula and the triangle inequality give, for every \(s\),
\[
 |\sigma_s(\widehat\Gamma_{1,n})-\sigma_s(\Gamma_1)|
 \leq\lVert\widehat\Gamma_{1,n}-\Gamma_1\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}.
\tag{2}
\]
At a fixed law \(\sigma_r(\Gamma_1)>0\), while \(\sigma_s(\Gamma_1)=0\) for \(s>r\).  Thus (1)--(2), \(t_n\downarrow0\), and \(\sqrt n\,t_n\to\infty\) imply
\[
 P(\widehat r_n=r)\longrightarrow1.
\tag{3}
\]

We next establish the required local regularity without choosing singular-vector signs.  Put \(M_1=\Gamma_1\Gamma_1^{\mathsf T}\).  Choose a closed contour \(\mathfrak c\) separating its \(r\) positive eigenvalues from zero.  Diagonalization of the real symmetric matrix \(M_1\) verifies that the active projector equals
\[
 P_S=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm i}\int_{\mathfrak c}(zI_p-M_1)^{-1}\,dz.
\tag{4}
\]
For a perturbation \(H\), the algebraic resolvent identity
\[
 (zI_p-M_1-H)^{-1}-(zI_p-M_1)^{-1}
 =(zI_p-M_1-H)^{-1}H(zI_p-M_1)^{-1}
\tag{5}
\]
shows first local Lipschitz continuity and, after one further substitution, a quadratic remainder after the linear term.  Hence the rank-\(r\) spectral projector is Fr\'{e}chet differentiable.  If \(O_0\) is any fixed orthonormal frame of \(S\), then
\[
 O(P)=PO_0\{O_0^{\mathsf T}PO_0\}^{-1/2}
\tag{6}
\]
is a differentiable orthonormal frame for every projector \(P\) close enough to \(P_S\).

If a frame is replaced by \(OU\) for an orthogonal \(U\), the quantities in \(\text{def:two-lag-inverse}\) transform as
\[
 R\mapsto U^{\mathsf T}R,\quad F\mapsto U^{\mathsf T}FU,\quad
 E\mapsto U^{\mathsf T}EU,\quad L^\star\mapsto U^{\mathsf T}L^\star U,\quad
 H^\star\mapsto U^{\mathsf T}H^\star U.
\tag{7}
\]
Therefore \(O(H^\star)^2R\), its skew part, \(A^\star\), and \(\mu^\star\) do not change.  Thus (6) may be used to differentiate the actual basis-invariant empirical inverse even if the measurable singular-value-decomposition rule in \(\text{def:rank-adaptive-estimator}\) chooses another frame.

At the population point every denominator is interior.  Indeed, \(RR^{\mathsf T}\succ0\), \(q=e^{-\beta\Delta}\in(0,1)\), and every eigenvalue of \(F\) is \(q\), so \(F-I_r\) is invertible.  Positive baselines imply \(\lambda_j=m_j/\Delta>0\).  Acyclicity gives \(A^p=0\), and hence
\[
 (I_p-A/\beta)^{-1}=\sum_{d=0}^{p-1}(A/\beta)^d.
\tag{8}
\]
Equations (4)--(8), continuity of matrix inversion, smoothness of \(-\Delta^{-1}\log q\) on \((0,1)\), and the finite polynomial defining \(L^\star\) show that the inverse before positive parts is locally Lipschitz.  The scalar map \(x\mapsto\max\{x,0\}\) is globally one-Lipschitz, so the entire recovery map is locally Lipschitz.  All strict conditions defining \(\mathcal V_n\) hold at the population point and persist in a neighborhood.  Equations (1) and (3), followed by \(\text{thm:two-lag-exact-recovery}\), therefore yield
\[
 \widehat\theta_n\longrightarrow(\beta,A,\mu),
 \qquad
 \sqrt n\lVert\widehat A_n-A\rVert_{\mathrm F}=O_P(1),
 \qquad
 P(\mathcal V_n^c)\longrightarrow0.
\tag{9}
\]
The sentinel is used only on the last event and hence does not affect these limits.

Let \(a_{\min}=\min\{A_{ij}:A_{ij}>0\}>0\); this minimum exists because \(p\) is finite and \(A\neq0\).  For all sufficiently large \(n\), \(a_n<a_{\min}/2\).  On
\[
 \lVert\widehat A_n-A\rVert_{\max}<\min\{a_n,a_{\min}/2\},
\tag{10}
\]
no absent edge is selected and no present edge is deleted.  By (9) and the stated condition \(\sqrt n\,a_n\to\infty\), the probability of the complement of (10) tends to zero.  This proves all fixed-law assertions.

Now fix \(p\), \(\Delta\), and \(\kappa\).  By \(\text{lem:bin-moment-limit}\), \(\sqrt n(\widehat\xi_n-\xi(\theta))\) is uniformly tight.  The uniform moment bound controls \(m,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2\), so the derivative of the centering map is uniformly bounded and
\[
 \sqrt n\lVert\widehat\eta_n-\eta(\theta)\rVert=O_{P_\theta}(1)
 \quad\text{uniformly in }\theta.
\tag{11}
\]
By \(\text{ass:rank-gap}\), every positive singular value is at least \(\kappa\).  Combining (2), (11), \(t_n\to0\), and \(\sqrt n\,t_n\to\infty\) gives
\[
 \sup_{\theta:P_\theta\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}
 P_\theta\{\widehat r_n\neq r(\theta)\}\longrightarrow0.
\tag{12}
\]

We expose all remaining uniform conditioning margins.  First,
\[
 \lambda_{\min}(RR^{\mathsf T})=\sigma_r(\Gamma_1)^2\geq\kappa^2.
\tag{13}
\]
Second, \(\text{ass:decay-margin}\) gives \(q\in[\kappa,1-\kappa]\).  The moment bound and (13) bound \(\lVert F\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\).  Since every eigenvalue of \(F\) is \(q\), including for repeated poles and singular excitation matrices,
\[
 |\det(F-I_r)|=(1-q)^r\geq\kappa^r.
\tag{14}
\]
For any \(r\times r\) matrix \(M\), the product of its singular values equals \(|\det M|\), so
\[
 \sigma_{\min}(M)\geq |\det M|\lVert M\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{-(r-1)}.
\tag{15}
\]
Equations (14)--(15) uniformly bound \((F-I_r)^{-1}\).  The finite logarithm has degree at most \(p-1\), and its derivative is uniformly bounded because both \(q^{-1}\) and \(F\) are bounded.  Assumption \(\text{ass:intensity-gap}\) gives \(\lambda_j\geq\kappa\) in every edge division.  Finally, \(\beta\geq\underline\beta=-\Delta^{-1}\log(1-\kappa)>0\), and \(\text{ass:parameter-envelope}\) with (8) gives
\[
 \lVert(I_p-A/\beta)^{-1}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
 \leq\sum_{d=0}^{p-1}(\kappa^{-1}/\underline\beta)^d.
\tag{16}
\]
Thus every strict validity condition persists on a neighborhood of a common radius, while (4)--(6) have a common derivative bound because the positive and zero spectra are separated by \(\kappa^2\).  Taking the maximum over the finitely many ranks \(1,\ldots,p\) gives a common local Lipschitz constant for the basis-invariant inverse.

Combining this common Lipschitz bound with (11)--(12) proves
\[
 \lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}
 \sup_{\theta:P_\theta\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}
 P_\theta\!\left[
 \sqrt n\{ |\widehat\beta_n-\beta|+
 \lVert\widehat A_n-A\rVert_{\mathrm F}+
 \lVert\widehat\mu_n-\mu\rVert_2\}>M\right]=0.
\tag{17}
\]
The exceptional sentinel contribution is bounded by the probability in (12) plus the probability that (11) leaves the common validity neighborhood, both uniformly negligible.  The final assertion about \(\widehat\Omega_n\) is exactly the Bartlett-HAC conclusion of \(\text{lem:bin-moment-limit}\) under \(\text{ass:hac-bandwidth}\).  No Gaussian law for the nonsmooth positive-part amplitude map has been used.
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} The theorem converts exact identification into a total measurable estimator and proves pointwise consistency plus uniform root-\(n\) tightness through a basis-invariant spectral projector. \textit{Gap.} The proof explicitly controls \(RR^{\mathsf T}\), \(q\), \(F-I_r\), intensity divisions, and \(I_p-A/\beta\), including singular excitation and repeated poles after random rank selection. \textit{Consumer.} Low-resolution alarm analysis obtains de-aliased decay, amplitudes, and baselines with an explicit abstention event rather than an undefined numerical inverse.

\begin{proposition}[prop:thresholded-uniform-support]\label{prop:thresholded-uniform-support}
Fix \(p\), \(\Delta\), and \(\kappa\in(0,1/2)\). If \(t_n,a_n\downarrow0\), \(\sqrt n\,t_n\to\infty\), and \(\sqrt n\,a_n\to\infty\), then the separately thresholded graph \(\widehat G_n=\{j\to i:\widehat A_{ij}>a_n\}\) satisfies \[\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}P_{\theta}\{\widehat G_n\neq G(A)\}\longrightarrow0.\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By \(\text{thm:rank-adaptive-estimation}\),
\[
 \sqrt n\lVert\widehat A_n-A\rVert_{\max}=O_{P_\theta}(1)
\tag{1}
\]
uniformly on \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)\), since the maximum norm is bounded by the Frobenius norm.  We convert \(\text{ass:edge-gap}\), which is stated for the signed jump, to a uniform amplitude margin.  Acyclicity gives \(A^p=0\) and therefore
\[
 \lambda=(I_p-A/\beta)^{-1}\mu
 =\sum_{d=0}^{p-1}(A/\beta)^d\mu.
\tag{2}
\]
On the margin class, \(\beta\geq\underline\beta=-\Delta^{-1}\log(1-\kappa)\), \(\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\kappa^{-1}\), and \(\lVert\mu\rVert_2\leq\kappa^{-1}\).  Hence
\[
 \max_j\lambda_j\leq\lVert\lambda\rVert_2
 \leq\Lambda_\kappa:=\kappa^{-1}
 \sum_{d=0}^{p-1}(\kappa^{-1}/\underline\beta)^d<\infty.
\tag{3}
\]
For a true edge \(j\to i\), acyclicity excludes \(i\to j\), and the skew-jump identity in \(\text{thm:two-lag-exact-recovery}\) gives \(Z_{ij}=A_{ij}\lambda_j\).  Thus \(\text{ass:edge-gap}\) and (3) imply
\[
 A_{ij}=|Z_{ij}|/\lambda_j\geq\kappa/\Lambda_\kappa.
\tag{4}
\]
For an absent directed edge, \(A_{ij}=0\).  For all sufficiently large \(n\), \(a_n<\kappa/(2\Lambda_\kappa)\).  On the event
\[
 \lVert\widehat A_n-A\rVert_{\max}
 <\min\{a_n,\kappa/(2\Lambda_\kappa)\},
\tag{5}
\]
there are neither false inclusions nor false exclusions, so \(\widehat G_n=G(A)\).  By (1) and the stated condition \(\sqrt n\,a_n\to\infty\), the probability of the complement of (5) tends to zero uniformly.  This proves the proposition.
\end{proof}
\par\smallskip\noindent \textit{Justification.} The proposition separates model selection from fixed-level confidence inversion and uses the correct condition \(\sqrt n\,a_n\to\infty\). \textit{Gap.} The signed-jump margin is converted to a uniform amplitude margin by bounding the stationary intensities through the finite nilpotent mean expansion. \textit{Consumer.} Analysts receive a support-consistent point graph without mistaking a fixed-level confidence decision for consistent selection.

\begin{theorem}[thm:simultaneous-graph-coverage]\label{thm:simultaneous-graph-coverage}
For fixed \(p\), \(\Delta\), and \(\kappa\in(0,1/2)\), the composite \(\zeta\circ\Phi_{\Delta,r(\theta)}\circ\mathcal C\), evaluated through the signed jump before the positive-part edge map, is Fr\'{e}chet differentiable at \(\xi(\theta)\). Let \[W(\theta)=D(\zeta\circ\Phi_{\Delta,r(\theta)}\circ\mathcal C)_{\xi(\theta)}\,\Omega(\theta)\,D(\zeta\circ\Phi_{\Delta,r(\theta)}\circ\mathcal C)_{\xi(\theta)}^{\mathsf T}.\] Under the derived geometric-mixing and eighth-moment lemmas and the long-run-covariance nonsingularity and bandwidth conditions of \(\text{thm:rank-adaptive-estimation}\), \[\sup_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}d_{\mathrm{BL}}\left(\mathcal L_{\theta}\left[\sqrt n\left\{\zeta(\widehat\theta_n)-\zeta(\theta)\right\}\right],\mathcal N\{0,W(\theta)\}\right)\longrightarrow0,\] and the direct composite estimator \(\widehat W_n\) in the corrected \(\text{def:hac-estimator}\) is uniformly consistent for \(W(\theta)\). Gaussian-maximum calibration with \(\widehat W_n\) yields \[\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}P_{\theta}\left\{\zeta_{ij}(\theta)\in I_{ij,n}\text{ for every }1\leq i<j\leq p\right\}\geq1-\alpha,\] and consequently \[\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{\theta:P_{\theta}\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}P_{\theta}\{G(A)\in\mathcal C_n(1-\alpha)\}\geq1-\alpha.\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let \(d=p(p-1)/2\), and let \(g_r\) map the raw moment vector to the \(d\) upper-triangular entries of the recovered signed jump \(Z^\star=K^\star-(K^\star)^{\mathsf T}\), using the rank-\(r\) image construction.  On \(\mathcal V_n\), the definitions give, for every \(i<j\),
\[
 \lambda_j^\star A_{ij}^\star-\lambda_i^\star A_{ji}^\star
 =\max\{Z_{ij}^\star,0\}-\max\{-Z_{ij}^\star,0\}
 =Z_{ij}^\star.
\tag{1}
\]
Moreover, \(\mu^\star=(I_p-A^\star/\beta^\star)\lambda^\star\), and invertibility of \(I_p-A^\star/\beta^\star\) is part of \(\mathcal V_n\); hence the intensity computed inside \(\zeta(\widehat\theta_n)\) is exactly \(\lambda^\star\).  On \(\mathcal V_n^c\), both \(\widehat z\) and \(\zeta(1,0,\mathbf1_p)\) are zero.  Therefore the total definitions satisfy
\[
 \widehat z=\zeta(\widehat\theta_n)
 =g_{\widehat r_n}(\widehat\xi_n)\quad\text{on }\mathcal V_n,
 \qquad
 \widehat z=\zeta(\widehat\theta_n)=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal V_n^c.
\tag{2}
\]

The contour-projector and polar-frame formulas (4)--(6) in the proof of \(\text{thm:rank-adaptive-estimation}\) show that the truncated active image has a differentiable local frame whenever the positive singular values are separated from zero.  In such a frame, \(F,q,\beta^\star,E,L^\star,H^\star,K^\star\), and \(Z^\star\) are compositions of multiplication, inversion at nonsingular matrices, the scalar logarithm on \((0,1)\), and a finite matrix polynomial.  Thus \(g_r\) is Fr\'{e}chet differentiable at each population point.  Identity (1) says that, on the open validity neighborhood, \(g_r=\zeta\circ\Phi_{\Delta,r}\circ\mathcal C\), including when a population signed-jump coordinate is zero.  This proves the differentiability claimed in the theorem; it neither requires nor asserts differentiability of either positive part separately.

The conditioning bounds (13)--(16) in \(\text{thm:rank-adaptive-estimation}\), \(\text{ass:rank-gap}\), \(\text{ass:decay-margin}\), \(\text{ass:parameter-envelope}\), and \(\text{ass:intensity-gap}\) give a common derivative bound and a common modulus of continuity.  There are only finitely many ranks.  Hence, on the uniformly likely event \(\{\widehat r_n=r(\theta)\}\cap\mathcal V_n\), Taylor expansion gives
\[
 \sqrt n\{\widehat z-\zeta(\theta)\}
 =J_z(\theta)\sqrt n\{\widehat\xi_n-\xi(\theta)\}+o_{P_\theta}(1),
 \qquad
 J_z(\theta)=D(\zeta\circ\Phi_{\Delta,r(\theta)}\circ\mathcal C)_{\xi(\theta)},
\tag{3}
\]
with a remainder uniform over \(\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)\).  Uniform rank and validity consistency follow from \(\text{thm:rank-adaptive-estimation}\).  Applying the bounded linear maps \(J_z(\theta)\) to the uniform Gaussian approximation in \(\text{lem:bin-moment-limit}\), then using (2)--(3), proves
\[
 \sup_{\theta:P_\theta\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}
 d_{\mathrm{BL}}\!\left(
 \mathcal L_\theta[\sqrt n\{\zeta(\widehat\theta_n)-\zeta(\theta)\}],
 \mathcal N\{0,W(\theta)\}\right)\longrightarrow0,
\tag{4}
\]
where \(W(\theta)=J_z(\theta)\Omega(\theta)J_z(\theta)^{\mathsf T}\).

We next verify the covariance used for calibration.  Write \(v_s=Y_s-\overline Y_n\) and \(N_n=n-2\).  The Bartlett estimator in \(\text{def:hac-estimator}\) is exactly
\[
 \widehat\Omega_n=\frac1{N_n}\sum_{s,t=0}^{N_n-1}
 \left(1-\frac{|s-t|}{b_n}\right)_+v_sv_t^{\mathsf T}.
\tag{5}
\]
For integer \(b_n\geq1\),
\[
 \left(1-\frac{|s-t|}{b_n}\right)_+
 =\frac1{b_n}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb Z}
 \mathbf1_{\{s\leq\ell<s+b_n\}}
 \mathbf1_{\{t\leq\ell<t+b_n\}},
\tag{6}
\]
a Gram representation.  Hence \(\widehat\Omega_n\succeq0\).  Its signed-score sandwich is positive semidefinite on \(\mathcal V_n\), and it is zero, hence positive semidefinite, on \(\mathcal V_n^c\).  By \(\text{lem:bin-moment-limit}\), \(\widehat\Omega_n\to\Omega(\theta)\) uniformly.  Derivative continuity, (3), and uniform validity then give, for every \(\varepsilon>0\),
\[
 \sup_{\theta:P_\theta\in\mathcal M_{\Delta,p}(\kappa)}
 P_\theta\{\lVert\widehat W_n-W(\theta)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}>\varepsilon\}
 \longrightarrow0.
\tag{7}
\]

The accepted optional full-parameter object is also fully explicit.  The event
\[
 \mathcal D_n=\mathcal V_n\cap
 \{Z_{ij}^\star\neq0\text{ for every }1\leq i<j\leq p\}
\tag{8}
\]
is measurable: \(\mathcal V_n\) is measurable by \(\text{def:rank-adaptive-estimator}\), every starred map is Borel on it, and (8) is a finite intersection of inverse images of \(\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}\).  On \(\mathcal D_n\), every positive-part coordinate is differentiable and the full sandwich \(\widehat V_n\) is defined; on \(\mathcal D_n^c\) it is the declared zero placeholder and the optional parameter set uses its full-space fallback.  Neither object enters (4) or graph calibration.

It remains to justify calibration when \(W(\theta)\) is singular.  Define the raw-moment direction
\[
 v_\theta=\{0,\operatorname{vec}(\Gamma_1),\operatorname{vec}(\Gamma_2)\}.
\]
Along \(\xi+s v_\theta\), the mean is fixed and both centered lag covariances are multiplied by \(1+s\).  Thus the active image is fixed, \(R\) is multiplied by \(1+s\), and direct cancellation in the formula for \(F\) gives \(F(s)=F\).  Consequently \(q,E,L^\star,H^\star\) are fixed while \(K^\star,Z^\star\), and \(g_r\) are multiplied by \(1+s\).  Therefore
\[
 J_z(\theta)v_\theta=\zeta(\theta).
\tag{9}
\]
